If the error isn't one of these, it returns -1. Otherwise, it proceeds.

4. The function relies on an assertion that might not catch all invalid inputs, allowing incorrect values to be returned.

Therefore, the code is not 100% secure and has vulnerabilities that could be exploited under certain conditions.

The code provided contains several potential vulnerabilities related to integer operations and bitwise manipulations. Here's a breakdown of the issues:

1. **Integer Overflow**: The multiplication of `levels` and `c` can easily overflow the integer limit, leading to undefined behavior. This is a critical security risk as it can cause unexpected results or crashes.

2. **Undefined Behavior with Shifts**: If `e` exceeds 24, the shift amount `(24 - e)` becomes negative, which is undefined in C. This could lead to unexpected results or crashes if `e` is not properly bounded.

3. **Bitwise Operations on Signed Integers**: The use of bitwise shifts on signed integers can cause sign extension issues, potentially leading to incorrect results. This could affect the quantization process and lead to incorrect return values.

4. **Reliance on Assertions**: While the function includes an `av_assert2` to check the validity of `v`, it does not cover all possible input ranges. This means that incorrect inputs could pass the assertion and return incorrect values, which might be exploited in certain contexts.
